 GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS 
G.1. Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)  
216. The proposal is a Category C in terms of environmental and social risks. ESS screening of the 
proposed project was undertaken against the GCF Guidelines for the Environmental and Social 
Screening of Activities Proposed under the Simplified Approval Process (SAP) during the design 
phase. The SAP ESS screening is substantially the same as the Program Environmental and Social 
Sustainability Management System (PESSMS) that Save the Children uses internally for similar 
projects. The objective of these guidelines is to apply best practice standards while reducing process 
times for micro and small-scale activities that promote and support certain scalable and 
transformational activities, typically activities qualifying as Category C. The project qualifies to use the 
SAP guidelines because the proposed actions are small-scale at the individual activity level, including: 
local community-based watershed and habitat management and rehabilitation; climate resilient 
agriculture (including soil and water conservation) and community forest management activities.  
217. In undertaking this screening, the potential risks and impacts have been considered that include direct 
and indirect, induced, long-term and cumulative impacts and have taken into account the proposed 
activities area of influence. SEAH provisions have been included in the project risk screening and its 
Environmental and Social Assessment and Residual Risk Management Plan (RRMP) Annex 6, to 
prevent and respond effectively to SEAH in a survivor-centred and gender-responsive way in 
accordance with the Green Climate Funds (GCF) Revised Environmental and Social Policy (ESP) 
(B.BM-2021/18). 
218. As per the SAP guidelines, the screening has been carried out at the pre-mitigation stage to enable 
the most serious potential impacts of the activities to be considered. While there are several 
categories of sub-projects proposed under VCCRP, as per GCF requirements, the risk category of the 
highest risk activity is applied to the entire project.  
219. Part A of the SAP ESS Screening is against a set of exclusion criteria. The exclusion criteria describe 
activities that have specific risk factors that would (i) raise the overall environmental and social risks of 
the proposed activities and would (ii) require more detailed assessment. Outcomes of Part A 
screening are shown below and verify that the exclusion criteria are not triggered by the proposed 
activities and their associated tasks and therefore the SAP can be used for the VCCRP.

G.2. Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)  
221. Existing inequality and disadvantage in Vanuatu are amplified by climate change. Access to 
resources, decision making, information and safety are all mediated through norms associated with 
gender, disability and age. In rural communities, agriculture and fisheries are family enterprises with 
different roles undertaken by men and women of different ages and abilities. However, men own the 
majority of resources and women’s access is based on fathers, husbands and sons. Decision-making 
positions in “kastom” governance as well as national and provincial government are almost exclusively 
male. At family level there is greater evidence of shared decision-making. Community development, 
church and market committees are all places where space has been created for women’s decision-
making and is starting to shift social norms. People with disability and children remain excluded from 
these structures. 
222. National agriculture and fisheries policies now recognize the value of and importance of engaging 
whole communities and families – women, men, children and youth of all ages and people with 
disabilities. However long-established practices of working with male heads of household, focusing on 
their roles in agriculture and fisheries are slow to change. Women’s roles in ensuring food security 
and value adding through marketing, food and handicraft production have not been a focus of climate 
change adaptations. Yet women’s ability to grow food, fish, collect marine resources, and access 
 
58 Vanuatu’s GCF Accreditation Process: Environment and Social Safeguards Analysis and Recommendations, USAID Climate Ready Project, December 2019

=== PAGE 99 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 97 
 
 
 
 
fresh water are all directly impacted by drought, heavy rainfall, high seas and depleting marine 
resources. Work burdens are increasing, and women face risks of gender-based violence travelling 
further to gardens and to collect water. Vanuatu already has high rates of gender-based violence 
which serves to underpin women’s exclusion from decision making, reinforces women’s unpaid care 
roles and limit women’s mobility. 
223. The project will provide opportunities for women, men, girls and boys of all ages to build their 
resilience to the impacts of climate change in ways that are meaningful to them. Comprehensive 
community and stakeholder engagement is the foundation on which locally-led adaptation is built. 
Under the project, all stakeholder engagement will have issues of gender and social inclusion and 
equity at their heart. CDCCCs will aim for gender equity in representation and decision making, and 
Community Adaptation Plans will be inclusively developed and address climate risk and resilience 
building from all community perspectives. The project will ensure that women and men have equal 
voice in the determination of community adaptation priorities and equally benefit from project 
activities. Activities implemented via the adaptation packages (Component 2) will work to address the 
specific adaptation needs of all community members, within the project’s scope, and (under Output 
2.4) focus exclusively on increasing women’s economic empowerment via supporting the 
establishment and operation of women-led enterprises which will help increase and diversify incomes. 
These needs and opportunities are further explored in the full GESI Assessment and Action Plan (at 
Annex 8).  
224. The GESI Action Plan notes that the project benefits women, people with disability and children and 
youth, as well as men. Project interventions will be targeted in ways that support women, people with 
disability and children and youth to access climate information, and resources and skills to adapt their 
livelihoods. To support lasting change the project will also address underlying social norms and 
behaviours associated with work roles and workload, leadership and violence. This means going 
beyond individual self-improvement activities for women, people with disability and youth to also 
transforming power dynamics at household, community, area and province levels that serve to 
reinforce inequality.  
225. A “twin track” approach to disability inclusion will be applied – working directly with people with 
disability, and also mainstreaming disability inclusion into the wider community and government 
systems. Vanuatu Society of Disabled People and Vanuatu Skills Partnership are key partners for 
building skills and training, while Vanuatu Disabled People’s Association (Santo) and Rainbow 
Theatre are important advocacy organizations.  
226. Youth will be engaged in project activities to ensure they benefit from adaptation actions and have a 
voice in adaptation planning and community-level decision making.  
227. The full gender and social inclusion assessment and project-level gender and social inclusion action 
plan is available in Annex 8. 
G.3. Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page) 
228. Fiduciary arrangements will follow a decentralized model, that involves the Accredited Entity (AE), 
Save the Children Australia, providing direct funding tranches separately to both co-Executing 
Entities, the Ministry of Climate Change and Save the Children Vanuatu, following endorsement of 
work plans and budgets by the Project Steering Committee and the NAB. Once funding is received by 
the Vanuatu National Government from the AE via the Reserve Bank of Vanuatu, a decentralized 
financial management approach will be utilized, where each separate government agency will receive 
its own budget / appropriation following established work plans and will be responsible for reporting to 
the Project Management Unit at the Ministry of Climate Change for all expenditure. Existing 
administrative structures for reporting against funds received would be distributed across pre-existing 
line Ministry structures located in the Corporate Service Units of each Ministry (as opposed to being 
centralized in one unit only). This is the approach used by other programs such as the European 
Union Budget Support, as it significantly reduces the overall administration costs of the program and 
aligns with the intent of the Vanuatu PFEM Act. The EEs will follow the AE rules and policies. This 
requirement will be stipulated in the sub agreements.

=== PAGE 100 ===
F 
 
GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 98 
 
 
 
 
229. Funding received by Save the Children Vanuatu would be used for implementation of its work plan 
and programming, including the engagement of procured parties, as approved by the Project Steering 
Committee and NAB. 
Transparency Implications 
230. A decentralized model allows for coding of project activities at a higher level in the chart of accounts 
and as a result the FMIS general reporting system would be able to produce the financial reports 
required without additional analysis and manipulation. 
231. This model also provides a better representation of project spending as expenses are shown 
alongside other sector spending, which increases transparency and understanding of how these funds 
are being utilized compared to funds from other donors and the Government budget. The funds would 
also be more visible to the responsible line Ministries who can view and manage the budget used for 
the project activities they are expected to deliver. 
Administrative burden 
232. A decentralized approach can be more administratively cumbersome as it entails multiple spending 
units across the Government, however, these units already exist within the Corporate Service Units of 
each Ministry. Therefore, no new structures or work units need to be established in terms of funds 
administration and the workload would be distributed across pre-existing line Ministry structures. This 
is the approach already in use by other programs such as the European Union Budget Support as it 
significantly reduces the overall administration costs of the project. 
Control 
233. As mentioned above, a decentralized model allows more efficient coding of the project activities in 
terms of their alignment with the overall “funds control” of the Government system. By spreading the 
funds allocation across Ministries the project can use higher parts of the coding structure (program, 
activity, and cost centre) for which there are strong financial controls already in place that would 
prevent overspending or any one component of the program adversely affecting another. 
Alignment with government and donor policies 
234. A decentralized model is aligned with Government and donor policies and allows for an accurate 
representation of project resources by sector and such reports could be produced using the pre-
existing government reporting tools and templates without the need to extract and manipulate data 
externally. It is also aligned with recent Government declarations to provide a greater alignment of 
donor and Government resources in terms of how they are operationalized and reported on by sector. 
Performance management/auditing 
235. A decentralized model enables the project to link seamlessly into the Vanuatu Government’s system 
of performance management and reporting. Under program budgeting, each agency of Government is 
required to publish a “narrative” that accompanies the budget report. This narrative forms part of the 
presentation of the budget to the Parliament, and importantly contains service delivery or output and 
outcome targets. Each agency is then obliged to report on these targets. Hence, the project will use a 
decentralized financial model to link into the performance management reporting of Government, 
thereby removing duplicate reporting. 
236. Annual audit of the financials of the project will be completed by SCA’s independent external auditor, 
PriceWaterhouseCoopers.  Two Internal audit reviews to be conducted by SCA’s in house Head of 
Internal audit in years 2 and 5 which will focus on reviewing compliance with SCA’s policies and 
procedures and GCF requirements. The Australian accounting standards which are aligned to IFRS 
will be used for project reporting.

Secretariat’s assessment of FP184 
Proposal name:

I. 
Overall assessment of the Secretariat  
1. 
The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following 
remarks: 
Strengths 
Points of caution 
The project offers an innovative approach to 
transforming local food systems and boosting 
resilience with long-term benefits  
None

unique opportunity to tailor solutions and 
long-term opportunities to local landscapes 
and ecosystems and their individual key

2. 
The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and 
conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum VII, titled “List of proposed 
conditions and recommendations”.  
II. 
Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment 
2.1 
Project background 
3. 
Vanuatu is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world to extreme weather events 
induced by climate change. It was ranked eighth in the 2020 Global Climate Risk Index and 
received the highest disaster risk score of 49.7 in the World Risk Report in 2020. The country 
has been suffering from increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, 
including heatwaves, flooding and tropical cyclones. Food systems and livelihoods in Vanuatu 
have been affected by these climate change impacts, as 80 per cent of the population depend on 
subsistence agriculture and are susceptible to rainfall variability and extreme rainfall. The food 
security of rural households who depend on coastal fisheries is also threatened by changes in 
the number, distribution and sizes of coral reef fish and invertebrate species brought about by 
climate change. 
4. 
The Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project aims to fill the adaptation 
deficit in 282 remote and rural communities across six provinces. It will do so by supporting 
access to and utilization of local-level climate information, integrating climate risks into 
community planning processes and implementing priority adaptation actions. The project will 
directly benefit 90,157 people in 28 Area Councils. These are the most vulnerable and socially 
marginalized people in Vanuatu, who will benefit through the implementation of the project’s

=== PAGE 105 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 105 
 
 
adaptation packages, including the adoption of climate-resilient agriculture and fisheries and 
the development of diversified or enhanced livelihoods resilient to flooding, storm surges, 
salinization, drought and heatwaves. The project will also indirectly benefit an additional 
110,000 rural people.   
5. 
The total project financing is USD 32,650,440, with a request for GCF grants of USD 
26,182,878. Co-financing will be provided by the Government of Australia of USD 4,625,040 in 
grants, USD 1,541,230 in grants by the Government of Vanuatu and USD 301,292 in grants by 
Save the Children Australia (SCA). The project is submitted under environmental and social 
safeguards category C. 
2.2 
Component-by-component analysis  
Component 1: Government, civil society and communities are strengthened to support local 
resilience to climate change impacts, including by providing access to climate information and 
early warning (total cost: USD 6.68 million; GCF cost: USD 6.02 million, or 90 per cent) 
6. 
Component 1 aims to strengthen the resilience of 254 local communities (90 per cent of 
the total number of target communities) to climate change impacts through establishing 
Community Disaster and Climate Change Committees (CDCCCs) that will guide adaptation and 
disaster preparation and response for the local communities. Under this component Community 
Adaptation Plans will be developed with CDCCCs and awareness raised among communities of 
climate change risks through targeted information, education and communication materials. It 
will extend climate information services and early warning systems to target communities via 
various available technologies, which will increase the technical competency of the communities 
in utilizing climate information services and early warning systems. 
7. 
Baseline assessment shows that CDCCCs are either non-existent or not fully functional in 
the target communities. However, as a result of the active engagement and strong 
determination of the Government of Vanuatu in its climate actions, their terms of reference, 
including their structure, roles and responsibilities, have already been defined within the 
National Disaster Management Office under the Ministry of Climate Change (MoCC). The 
proposed activity of strengthening CDCCCs is therefore deemed to be appropriate in filling the 
financial gap for operationalizing the committees, providing an opportunity to increase 
community-level ownership in managing disaster risks. Although the Government of Vanuatu’s 
in-kind co-financing will support this activity, it will be important to secure resources to 
continue the committees’ activities after project completion. 
8. 
The activities related to climate information services and early warning systems have 
been extensively discussed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) in view of the 
potential overlap of proposed activities with those of approved GCF project FP035, “Climate 
Information Services for Resilient Development Planning in Vanuatu” (Van-CIS-RDP). Detailed 
mapping exercises have been conducted at the activity level by the AE in aligning the activities 
between the two projects. In essence, output 1.3 in the proposed project will enable wider 
dissemination of the climate information generated by the Van-CIS-RDP project at the 
community level. This includes utilizing outputs from the Van-CIS-RDP project as an input to the 
proposed project’s community training and facilitating access to the Van-CIS-RDP project’s 
climate information services on an ongoing basis. 
9. 
The appropriateness and necessity of the proposed component has been justified in that 
the awareness-raising and climate information services provision under this component covers 
all types of beneficiaries, including government (Area Councils), civil society and communities. 
Activities under this component should be closely linked to the capacity-building under 
component 3, especially the knowledge generation and dissemination for the target 
communities.

=== PAGE 106 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 106 
 
 
Component 2: Scalable, locally appropriate actions are implemented to meet community 
adaptation needs to create climate-resilient, sustainable development pathways (total cost: USD 
14.56 million; GCF cost: USD 13.89 million, or 95 per cent) 
10. 
Component 2 will strengthen agriculture and fisheries systems through nature-based 
solutions and local value chain improvement. The first output includes the reforestation and 
restoration of upland forest areas and shoreline vegetation, as well as the protection of critical 
watershed areas and marine habitats, leading to a total of 11,600 ha sites under better 
management. This will be done through distributing 500,000 tree seedlings and building at least 
254 erosion management demonstration sites. The second output will support 17,072 
smallholder farmer households in carrying out climate-resilient agriculture techniques in 
household plots through training and the provision of seeds and seedlings. 
11. 
This component is structured as an adaptation package, with a suite of adaptation 
activities in the agriculture and fisheries sectors. Target communities will be able to select the 
most appropriate and effective options within the package to meet their needs on the ground. 
Given the dispersed locations of the target communities in different Area Councils, the proposed 
approach is deemed adequate and the most feasible approach in delivering tailored adaptation 
solutions for the target livelihoods. While the exact adaptation interventions have not been 
determined for each community at the project appraisal stage, the proposal still manages to set 
overall output and outcome targets, as provided in the logical framework. The package has been 
designed based on wide stakeholder consultations and the targets may change depending on 
the needs of the communities. 
12. 
Under this component, GCF grants will be used to generate tangible outcomes to address 
food insecurity in the agriculture and fisheries sectors. The component focuses on the supply 
side of agricultural inputs, tools and techniques; to maximize upscaling potential, mechanisms 
that would incentivize target beneficiaries to adopt such inputs and techniques are required. As 
the priority has been given to addressing food insecurity due to climate change, such incentive 
mechanisms and business models have not been incorporated into the design of the project. It 
would be advisable to discuss, design and develop them as part of the knowledge products in 
component 3. 
13. 
The proposed agriculture and water conservation techniques are well-known and 
recognized agricultural practices that will produce ‘no regrets’ results. These techniques are 
based on a comprehensive stocktake of existing and traditional agricultural practices and crop 
calendars. As for the requested GCF grant funding, the proposal provides sound justification for 
the request of full concessionality, supported by the financial analysis, which shows marginal 
rate of return and net present value. 
Component 3: Institutional adaptive capacity is enhanced by building adaptive governance 
systems at the local level and enhancing local-provincial-national linkages (total cost: USD 8.51 
million; GCF cost: USD 3.67 million, or 43 per cent) 
14. 
Component 3 aims to build the capacity of provincial and national actors, as well as local 
authorities, to integrate climate risks into their governance, planning and budgeting processes. 
Based on climate risk analysis and vulnerability mapping for each Area Council, a tailored social 
protection system will be established, including a cash transfer system, to protect the most 
vulnerable population against climate change. The second part of this component is to generate 
and disseminate knowledge management products based on lessons learned and best practices 
gleaned through this project.  
15. 
A concrete financial exit strategy is provided only to a limited extent. The proposed 
component offers a good opportunity to design and mainstream a financial exit strategy that is 
viable in the local context and the context of projected climate impacts, supported by a cash 
transfer system and business plans.

=== PAGE 107 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 107 
 
 
16. 
The proposed component is assessed to be adequate and reasonable, given the 
importance of sharing knowledge and good practices across communities and provinces. 
Considering the remoteness of the islands from each other, expensive travel and high costs of 
goods and services production, the most cost-efficient way should be used to disseminate the 
knowledge and know-how through established mechanisms, including CDCCCs.  
Project management (total cost: USD 1.47 million; GCF cost: USD 1.17 million, or 80 per cent)  
17. 
The GCF portion of the project management cost is about 4.7 per cent of the total 
requested GCF funding and is compliant with the GCF policy on fees. 
III. 
Assessment of performance against investment criteria 
3.1 
Impact potential  
 
 
  
                               Scale: N/A  
18. 
 The project targets the most vulnerable and marginalized communities in Vanuatu 
covering a vast area of islands, including some of the most remote islands in the Vanuatu 
archipelago covering all six provinces. A total of 90,157 direct beneficiaries, or 33 per cent of the 
population, will become more resilient and achieve diversified livelihoods through the 
sustainable use of the adaptation packages and access to climate information services fostered 
by the project. The packages and the information services will enable the target communities to 
diversify their livelihoods and make them less vulnerable to shocks and hazards as well as less 
prone to future shocks caused by cyclones.  
19. 
The project will further reduce food insecurity and malnutrition by diversifying the 
existing farming and food systems by promoting highly diversified local food products that will 
provide communities with a very varied, healthy, nutritious and sustainable diet. 
20. 
The project offers a unique platform for scaling up the best practices fostered by the 
adaptation packages, the strengthened resilience and the enhanced capacity at both government 
and community level. This will be ensured by an innovative project design adapting the 
packages and support to the specific needs of the circumstances and challenges of each 
community group through government and national partners.  
3.2 
Paradigm shift potential  
  
  
 
 
        Scale: N/A  
21. 
The Vanuatu community-based climate resilience project fosters innovative solutions to 
meet the current climate challenges faced by communities on a daily basis and empowers them 
to become less vulnerable and build a solid platform to further diversify their livelihoods and 
adapt to future challenges, including shocks and hazards from cyclones.  
22. 
By combining the adaptation packages, well designed and adjusted to each of the 
provinces, islands and local circumstances, farming and food systems, with access to detailed 
and continuous climate information and built capacity across all levels of Vanuatuan society, the 
project will be able to improve the current situation for the poorest and most vulnerable 
population groups. Furthermore, it will offer a long-term solution to the continued 
empowerment of communities across the archipelago in adapting to future challenges and 
shocks and strengthen local food systems to provide healthy and nutritious diets as well as 
fostering rural entrepreneurship. 
23. 
The adaptation packages offer a unique platform to cater to the demands, challenges, 
opportunities and investment options of each community and its local landscapes and food 
systems.

=== PAGE 108 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01

3.3 
Sustainable development potential  
  
 
        Scale: N/A 
24. 
The project empowers communities through capacity-building, ensuring better access to 
education services and the sustainable management of ecosystem services and enabling 
communities to diversify their food systems and create jobs and growth at the local level as well 
through the adaptation packages. This will allow communities to strengthen and diversify their 
household incomes and help to create robust productive landscapes.  
25. 
The project will further boost health conditions for targeted communities through 
better access to healthy and diversified diets with locally produced and processed food, and this 
access and processing will continue to be expanded and take a new direction throughout 
implementation and beyond.  
26. 
Furthermore, the project fosters strong gender mainstreaming across the activities, 
ensuring equal participation in decision-making across the community groups and at the 
provincial level.  
3.4 
 Needs of the recipient  
 
 
  
                    Scale: N/A 
27. 
The project targets the most needy, vulnerable and poorest communities across the 
archipelago. The targeted communities are also the most marginalized.  
28. 
Coronavirus disease 2019 has had devastating impacts on the local economies across 
the six provinces, with market access blocked to the local communities, not to mention the 
collapse of the tourism sector.  
29. 
Vanuatu regularly experiences category 5 tropical cyclones, causing significant damage 
to local infrastructure and food systems and presenting a challenge to the government in 
financing the rebuilding of multiple damaged areas.  
30. 
The project will build the capacity of government across all levels and in parallel with 
empowering communities to better withstand such shocks and hazards and boost local food 
systems to be more productive, resilient and long term oriented.   
3.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
  
 
        Scale: N/A 
31. 
The project objectives and structure are fully aligned with all strategic frameworks of 
the country.  
32. 
The first nationally determined contribution of Vanuatu underlined the key priority of 
adaptation solutions. The country’s national adaptation programme of action also focuses on 
adaptation, pointing to five priority areas headed by agriculture and food security.  
33. 
The updated nationally determined contribution zooms in on quantifiable adaptation 
targets seeking to further boost resilience in the agriculture and water sectors. Agriculture is 
targeted as a main strategy with the focus on household income and nutrition needs in a 
changing climate.  
34. 
National policies and strategies such as the National Sustainable Development Plan also 
point directly to the key priority of building adaptation solutions that focus on food and 
nutrition. In addition, the Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy 2016–2030 
outlines the key importance of adaptation solutions.

IV. 
Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies 
4.1 
Environmental and social safeguards 
36. 
The project is classified as environmental and social safeguards category C. The project 
has developed an environmental and social assessment and residual risk management plan that 
provides for screening of proposed activities and management of potential minimal 
environmental and social residual risks and impacts. The environmental and social co-benefits 
include increased access to locally available food supplies; enhanced ecosystem services 
through reforestation, erosion control and shoreline protection; and increased access to 
education as a result of the enhanced capability to adapt to extreme climate events through 
better planning and implementation of disaster risk reduction strategies. The AE has requested 
minor revisions and clarifications of the plan, which will need to be addressed prior to Board 
circulation.  
37. 
The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B.BM-2021/18 
requires safeguarding from sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed 
activities. As the Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project aims to support highly 
vulnerable remote and rural communities across all six provinces of Vanuatu, where custom 
laws and practices are predominately administrated by male chiefs in rural areas, the SEAH 
risks associated with the project need to be addressed.  
38. 
The project has incorporated screening and management of SEAH-related potential risks 
and impacts in its relevant safeguards instruments, such as the Residual Risk Management Plan. 
As SEAH stems from unequal relations and power dynamics resulting in a culture of 
discrimination and privilege, the project examines such risks with regard to the project 
workforce and affected communities. The project considers that project staff and consultants 
spending time in communities, including occasionally overnight, will have influence or relative 
power over the distribution of project inputs where community members may not be aware of 
or able to identify and report any SEAH incident.  
39. 
To resolve the SEAH risks associated with the unequal power dynamics, the project has 
identified relevant measures in the Residual Risk Management Plan to mitigate and manage the 
SEAH risks and impacts by providing awareness-raising and training to build capacities for both 
its workforce and affected communities on the SEAH policy and SEAH-related procedure for 
monitoring and reporting and the project grievance redress mechanism. The project also plans 
to identify and equip community child protection and SEAH focal points to monitor the conduct 
of project staff during project implementation and report concerns or incidents as they arise. To 
ensure that the project-specific grievance redress mechanism responds to SEAH incidents, the 
project will train staff to handle sensitive disclosures and make referrals to appropriate service 
providers. 
40. 
The project identifies SEAH mitigation measures, including integrating SEAH into staff 
induction and training, reflecting SEAH prevention and response in the planning and initiation 
of community-based activities and monitoring SEAH risks and incidents throughout project 
implementation. The project considers that putting the SEAH prevention and response 
measures into place will not imply additional costs to the project as they are seen or costed as 
part of the project design.

=== PAGE 110 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 110 
 
 
41. 
The SEAH safeguarding undertaken by the AE is aligned with the SEAH provisions of the 
revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy. The AE is required to take all necessary measures 
to ensure such alignment to prevent and respond effectively to SEAH in a survivor-centred and 
gender-responsive way.  
4.2 
Gender policy 
42. 
The AE provided a gender assessment and action plan with the funding proposal and 
therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy. 
43. 
Vanuatu’s constitution provides rights to live freely without discrimination on the basis 
of sex. The country prepared the second national gender equality policy to enhance women’s 
economic status, mainstream gender equality into national sectoral policies, increase women’s 
participation in leadership and decision-making and foster gender-responsive community-
driven solutions to climate and disaster resilience. Vanuatu is a signatory to the Convention on 
the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. In addition, it has signed related 
regional commitments such as the Pacific Leaders Gender Equality Declaration. National 
institutional arrangements include the Department of Women’s Affairs situated in the Ministry 
of Justice and Community Services. The department is responsible for policy and programme 
development and supporting mainstreaming of gender into other government departments; 
however, it has limited technical and financial capacity at the national and provincial level to 
deliver on its mandate adequately.  
44. 
Social norms shape gender matters by giving men control over women’s behaviour and 
privileges to make decisions in traditional settings. Women have limited access to resources 
such as land, which is mediated through husbands and sons. Caring for family members, 
managing food gardens, fishing and water collection, handicraft work and selling produce at 
markets are roles assigned to women. Men have greater mobility and can be engaged in 
employment outside the home, including in urban centres and other countries.  
45. 
Women and men are active participants and producers in the fisheries and agriculture 
sectors. They grow different crops and use different levels of technology in agriculture. Women 
lead in weeding, planting and maintaining crops closer to home, while men take on clearing and 
ploughing land, including use of machinery. Women’s gender roles restrict their access to 
training and extension initiatives. Extension services and enterprise development training 
usually target male heads of household, thereby valuing men’s work over that of women. Deep 
water fishing with larger vessels for sale is a male-dominated activity, while women undertake 
coastal gleaning and fishing from canoes for food security, and aquaculture and fish processing 
to generate income. Although women participate in fishing their contributions are undervalued. 
The gender assessment also describes challenges faced by people with disabilities and the 
compounded issues of women with disabilities in Vanuatu. People with disabilities are targeted 
for stakeholder engagement, training and inclusion in committees. 
46. 
Community meetings, key informant interviews and women’s focus group discussions 
undertaken in the preparation phase of the project identified challenges associated with 
adapting to climate change and responding to natural disasters, the limited inclusion of women 
in community committees and the need for income generation. The following priorities were 
highlighted, especially by women: training on agricultural practices, value addition and financial 
literacy, food security and income diversification. Even though these priorities were identified, 
the gender assessment does not clearly describe the opportunities presented by the project to 
address them. Nonetheless, they are reflected in the gender action plan. 
47. 
The gender action plan submitted with the proposal contains activities aligned with 
those of the project, performance indicators, baseline, sex-disaggregated targets, timelines and 
responsibilities. The project aims to address the limited inclusion of women in community

=== PAGE 111 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 111 
 
 
structures by targeting a gender balance in CDCCCs to improve women’s participation in 
leadership and decision-making positions at the community level. Project benefits will be 
accessible to both women and men and sex-disaggregated targets are included for each activity. 
Although the funding proposal has outlined in detail the activities that will be supported by the 
project, the gender action plan describes them broadly as project-provided resources which will 
be accessible to women and men equitably. The gender action plan includes opportunities for 
women and men to participate in training on climate-resilient agriculture, canoe making and 
value addition skills. This responds to issues raised in stakeholder engagement during the 
project’s preparation and the gender assessment. Furthermore, inputs will be provided in the 
form of seeds, seedlings, simple agricultural tools, food processing and preservation 
technologies and fishing equipment. Other project benefits include food processing and 
preservation equipment such as solar food dryers and freezers to support food security. Needs 
and priorities identified in the consultation process to deliver income-generating opportunities 
to enhance resilience to climate change are reflected in the gender action plan through the 
support to market access provided to women-led producer groups which will be established for 
bringing agricultural and fisheries goods and value addition. 
48. 
Implementation arrangements include a gender equality and social inclusion adviser for 
the project who will be included in the project management unit to supervise and guide the 
execution of the action plan. The Department of Women’s Affairs will be included in the project 
steering committee. Executing entities (EEs) will also participate in the implementation of all 
the activities in the action plan. In addition, costs allocated from the project’s budget are 
included for each activity listed in the action plan.  
49. 
The AE is required to collect sex-disaggregated data on all activities where communities 
will benefit from project-provided resources and report accordingly to GCF as per the action 
plan. 
4.3 
Risks  
4.3.1. 
Overall programme assessment (medium risk) 
 
50. 
This is an adaptation project that seeks to support vulnerable rural communities in 
Vanuatu by implementing targeted adaptation actions and facilitating decentralized governance 
structures that enable rural populations to adapt to changing climate trends and reduce disaster 
risk. GCF is requested to provide USD 26.3 million in grants. Additional co-financing will come 
from the Government of Australia (USD 4.6 million grant), the Government of Vanuatu (USD 1.5 
million in-kind contribution) and the AE (USD 30,000 grant for project management costs).   
51. 
Vanuatu is a small island developing State. Moreover, given the public nature of the 
proposed activities and the difficulty of access to finance for community-based adaptation, the 
AE deems it appropriate to request grant funding.  
4.3.2. 
Accredited entity/executing entity’s capability to execute the current programme 
(medium risk) 
52. 
SCA is the AE of this project. It is a non-governmental organization based in Australia 
that operates internationally through its network. Globally, SCA is implementing a portfolio of 
over 100 resilience-related projects and programmes valued at more than USD 200 million, 
including those with explicit objectives to reduce climate and disaster risks, as well as to 
increase adaptive capacity and speed recovery from shocks and stresses. Specifically, SCA has 
implemented adaptation projects in countries including the Philippines, Bangladesh and 
Vanuatu.

=== PAGE 112 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 112 
 
 
53. 
The project will be co-executed by two entities, namely, the Government of Vanuatu, 
acting through MoCC, and Save the Children Vanuatu (SCV). MoCC will retain overall 
responsibility for the implementation of the project; it will be the lead EE for all outputs and 
activities. Both entities have experience in managing and delivering climate change programmes 
across Vanuatu at a range of scales and from a variety of funding sources; for example, MoCC is 
the co-EE for the Van-CIS-RDP project referred to in paragraph 8 above as well as the EE for a 
number of donor-funded projects, including via the Global Environment Facility. SCV is a 
separate legal entity that is managed by SCA and utilizes the SCA systems and processes. The AE 
has completed an initial partnership assessment for MoCC, which identified no red flag risks. 
However, an organizational review of SCV highlighted challenges in areas such as capacity and 
procedures. SCA expects remedial actions to address the identified shortcomings to be 
concluded in early 2022. 
4.3.3. 
Project-specific execution risks (medium risk) 
54. 
Long-term sustainability: two out of the three major components in this project will be 
dedicated to awareness-raising and/or capacity-building in order to foster understanding of 
climate change impacts and develop local adaptation plans, strengthen the capacity of 
government officials and incorporate climate risk analysis and financing strategies into the 
planning process. It may take time for such support to eventually translate into concrete actions 
within communities. The lag may affect the long-term sustainability of the project.   
55. 
Financing risk: multiple aspects of the proposed exit strategy hinge on the fact that 
financial resources from the national government will be allocated to community-based 
adaptation activities. However, the national government does not currently have sufficient 
funding, given the political complexities with budget cycles and approvals. At the subnational 
level, little to no funding is invested in community-based adaptation as this is not featured in the 
planning process. Although this project is envisaged to raise awareness and influence the 
central allocation of funds, subsequent funding to ensure the sustainability of community 
resilience measures could become a challenge.   
56. 
Economic analysis: a cost–benefit analysis returns a project-level economic internal rate 
of return of -1 per cent. The benefits are estimated by valuing the avoided damage, erosion and 
carbon emissions, as well as calculating production and income increase for agricultural 
activities. The negative internal rate of return indicates that the total costs outweigh the 
envisaged economic benefits, thus giving rise to questions about the viability and effectiveness 
of GCF investment in this project.  
4.3.4. 
GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk) 
57. 
In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains 
non-material and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single 
proposal. 
4.3.5. 
Compliance risk (medium risk) 
58. 
Vanuatu is not the subject of any current targeted United Nations Security Council 
resolutions or sanctions regimes. 
59. 
SCA, as the AE, confirmed that no project activities will be undertaken in a jurisdiction 
which is subject to or affected by United Nations Security Council resolutions. Furthermore, no 
individual or entity that is listed on any United Nations Security Council sanctions list, including 
the United Nations Consolidated Sanctions List, will be involved in any manner with the project 
or its activities, either as a counterparty, implementer or beneficiary.

=== PAGE 113 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 113 
 
 
60. 
SCA completed partnership assessments in October 2021 for its EEs, namely SCV and 
the Government of Vanuatu represented by MoCC, as follows: 
(a) 
SCV: the data collected for the assessment included a review of policies and procedures, 
summary findings of an audit completed by the SCA Global Assurance team in 2021 and 
discussions with the SCV team. The assessment found that SCV overall has strong 
systems, capacity and experience to implement the scope of work outlined in the 
funding proposal. No red flag risks were identified per the Initial Partnership 
Assessment (IPA); and 
(b) 
MoCC: the data collected for the assessment included a review of related policies, a 2020 
Public Finance Management Assessment by the Australian Department of Foreign 
Affairs and Trade for the Department of Education and the Government of Vanuatu 
overall, and discussions with MoCC staff. The assessment found that MoCC has the 
experience and expertise to successfully carry out the scope of work outlined in the 
project. Owing to limited access to MoCC staff during the review period, the data 
compiled by SCA on key areas of the MoCC financial systems are not fully complete and 
will require additional information gathering in collaboration with MoCC partners to fill 
gaps relating to controls, reporting, planning and staffing. Similarly, while SCA reviewed 
the Ministry of Finance and Economic Management and MoCC procurement guidelines, 
which were deemed adequate as outlined in their procurement manuals, it was unable 
to confirm how these policies and procedures are followed on a day-to-day basis. While 
the review identified no red flag risks, SCA will continue to collaborate with MoCC and 
plans to update the Initial Partnership Assessment (IPA) on an ongoing basis throughout 
project delivery and support MoCC in addressing any weakness as identified. 
61. 
SCA confirmed a broad array of control mechanisms established to address money-
laundering and terrorism financing risks, including: 
(a) 
Anti-Money Laundering and Countering the Financing of Terrorism Policy;  
(b) 
Regular vetting processes carried out for staff, partners and suppliers, in line with the 
SCA Staff and Partner/Supplier Vetting Guidelines; 
(c) 
Whistle-Blower Policy and Procedure; 
(d) 
Incident reporting mechanism for receiving and managing reports of suspected or 
alleged fraud, corruption or dishonesty, including anti-money-laundering and 
countering the financing of terrorism; 
(e) 
Reference and background checks carried out for new staff;  
(f) 
Partner assessment completed for all delivery partners and updated regularly; 
(g) 
Participatory programme design and implementation, including by the Government of 
Vanuatu and SCV; 
(h) 
Embedded Program Management Unit, which is jointly recruited by SCA; 
(i) 
Active Technical Working Group and Project Steering Committee approving 
disbursements; 
(j) 
Strengthened government financial management and procurement processes; 
(k) 
Financial management and procurement assessment conducted during 
effectiveness/start-up and regularly thereafter; 
(l) 
Audits and spot checks in relevant ministries; 
(m) 
Monthly budget v actual expenditure reports; 
(n) 
Rigorous background check on programme staff;

(o)

Similarly, SCA confirmed a broad array of control mechanisms established to address

(a) 
Fraud, Bribery and Anti-Corruption Policy;

63. 
SCA confirmed that it does not to distribute or disburse to beneficiaries, either directly 
or indirectly, cash, vouchers or commodities. However, it advised that the project plans to 
distribute low-value, simple agricultural and offshore fishing equipment to targeted households. 
The project will also distribute food processing and preservation equipment (solar dryers and 
freezers) to a number of communities. This will be carried out as follows: 
(a) 
The agricultural and fishing equipment will be distributed direct to households via 
community-level agreements under the Community Adaptation Plan in each targeted 
community (which will be developed through a collaborative stakeholder engagement 
process, endorsed by community and approved by the EEs). Distribution will be 
undertaken by relevant government departments (Department of Agriculture and Rural 
Development [DARD], Vanuatu Fisheries Department [VFD] and Department of Local 
Authorities [DLA]); and 
(b) 
The food processing and preservation equipment will be distributed to community-level 
producer groups identified via the Community Adaptation Plan process. Again, 
distribution will be via relevant government departments and approved by the EEs 
through the Community Adaptation Plans. 
64. 
SCA advised that the project will provision limited materials and technology to CDCCCs, 
co-operatives and other established and government accredited institutions which will limit 
diversion or misuse. SCA confirmed that, for the materials and technology that are distributed, 
the project will proactively engage with CDCCCs to establish community buy-in, increase 
knowledge of proper use and regularly publicize accountability mechanisms such as the 
whistle-blower, complaint and grievance redress mechanisms. Project monitoring will include 
checking on the presence and use of any distributed materials districted by the project.

=== PAGE 115 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01

65. 
SCA confirmed that its Whistle-blower policy was provided during accreditation and 
applies to all SCA staff, SCV staff and partner organizations. Supplemental to the whistle-blower 
policy, SCA maintains a complaints process that is accessible via phone, email and post. 
66.

4.5 
Results monitoring and reporting 
72. 
This is an adaptation project focusing on supporting highly vulnerable rural 
communities across Area Councils in Vanuatu to increase their resilience to climate variability 
and change by implementing targeted adaptation actions and facilitating decentralized 
governance structures that enable rural populations to adapt to changing climate trends and 
reduce disaster risk. The project’s adaptation packages provide a menu of actions to support 
households and communities in addressing key drivers of climate change vulnerability, 
minimize exposure and sensitivity and build resilience to unavoidable impacts. 
73. 
The project will reach and increase the adaptive capacity of 200,157 beneficiaries, out of 
which 90,157 are direct beneficiaries and 110,000 are indirect beneficiaries. It is the second 
GCF project supported in Vanuatu and will have strong synergies and build upon the work of the 
Van-CIS-RDP project.  
74. 
This is the first GCF project developed under the new GCF Integrated Results 
Management Framework. It is fully aligned with the requirements of the framework in relation 
to results management and uses the latest GCF funding proposal template.  
75. 
Annex 5 needs to add the key milestones and deliverables for each activity.  
76. 
Annex 11 is still to be updated in line with the Integrated Results Management 
Framework.  
4.6 
Legal assessment  
77. 
The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 20 
December 2019 (the “AMA”), and it became effective on 20 May 2020. 
78. 
The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion of its General Counsel dated 12 
August 2021 confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and 
authority to implement the project  
79. 
The proposed project will be implemented in Vanuatu. The GCF has signed a bilateral 
agreement on privileges and immunities with Vanuatu; however, such agreement is still 
pending its entry into force. This means that the privileges and immunities are only effectively 
accorded to GCF upon the agreement entering into effect. Therefore, until such moment, 
amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, 
which risks need to be further assessed.  
80. 
The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity 
Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress 
activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with 
relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the 
GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and 
expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

GCF/B.32/02/Add.01
