Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of 
FP184 
Proposal name: 
Vanuatu community-based climate resilience project (VCCRP) 
Accredited entity: 
Save the Children Australia  
Country/(ies): 
Vanuatu 
Project/programme size: 
Small 
I. 
Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel 
1.1 
Impact potential                                                                                  Scale: N/A 
1. 
This funding proposal for a small project in environmental and social safeguards 
category C is submitted by Vanuatu, a small island developing State1 in the Pacific, with 65 
inhabited islands and a population last recorded at 272,000. This is an adaptation project, with 
Save the Children Australia as the accredited entity (AE) and two co-executing entities – the 
Government of Vanuatu’s Ministry of Climate Change and Save the Children Vanuatu. The total 
cost of the funding proposal is USD 32.65 million, as detailed in paragraph 29 below. 
2. 
. Current available evidence, combining observed historical trends with future 
projections, indicates that a number of significant climate change challenges face communities 
in Vanuatu. Projected future trends in three key climate parameters are: 1) temperature: air and 
sea (increased minimum, mean and maximum daily temperatures and occurrence of events 
such as heatwaves); 2) rainfall patterns (increased duration of dry periods and changing 
frequency and intensity of extreme rainfall and rainfall associated with El Niño/Southern 
Oscillation); and 3) tropical cyclones (increased intensity, with extreme wind and waves, and 
intense rainfall and flooding). Category 5 cyclones in 2015 and 2020 caused loss of life and 
extensive damage to economic activities; the funding proposal shows that over the 42-year 
period to 2010, 101 tropical cyclones developed or crossed into the Vanuatu Exclusive 
Economic Zone, although the AE has not demonstrated a historical trend in relation to cyclone 
intensity. 
3. 
With about 80 per cent of the population of this Pacific island nation dependent on 
subsistence agriculture, climate change poses a significant risk to Vanuatu’s agriculture sector 
and food security. Agricultural activities in Vanuatu are particularly susceptible to climate 
change induced changes in rainfall patterns, extreme rainfall leading to flooding, droughts, 
heatwaves, salinization processes, increases in evapotranspiration, seasonal variations and 
reduction in freshwater availability. Under a Representative Concentration Pathway 4.5 
scenario, soil moisture is expected to decrease significantly, with potential increased 
evapotranspiration, indicating significant stress on non-irrigated crops. These climate impacts 
are exacerbated by soil erosion and loss of soil fertility due to deforestation and environmental 
degradation. Prolonged and intense rainfall damages seedlings and encourages conditions that 
promote diseases and pests. Droughts create additional thermal stress on plants. Projected air 
temperature increases are likely to reach the maximum heat tolerance thresholds of some 
staple crops and induce heat stress and crop failure, especially in traditional crops such as 
 
1 Vanuatu graduated out of least developed country status in 2020.

=== PAGE 118 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 118 
 
 
 
cassava, taro and yam. Communities on Torres islands, South Santo, South Malekula, Central 
Pentecost, Epi, Erromango, Aniwa and Aneityum, in particular, have reported impacts from 
increased temperatures and droughts on declining crop yields and lowered livestock 
productivity during the 2015–2016 El Niño period. Coastal fisheries also contribute significantly 
to food security, with 60 per cent of rural households engaging in coastal fisheries activities, 
which, already at critical risk from non-climatic pressures such as overfishing and habitat 
degradation from pollution, are further threatened by climate change. Changes in ocean 
temperature and acidity are expected to impact on coral reef fish and invertebrate species in 
Vanuatu by altering their metabolic rates, growth, reproduction and survival, resulting in 
changes to species abundance, distribution and sizes that may reduce catches and food security. 
4. 
Despite investments by the government and its technical and financial partners in the 
agriculture and fisheries sectors in response to these threats, more comprehensive, integrated 
adaptation measures are required, firmly rooted in communities’ needs, including 
strengthening early warning to vulnerable communities of both slow and sudden onset 
disasters intensified by climate change. The project’s goal statement is that IF vulnerable 
remote and rural communities in Vanuatu access and utilize locally relevant climate 
information, integrate climate risks into community planning processes and are supported in 
implementing priority adaptation actions, THEN they will be more resilient to the impacts of 
increasing climate variability, extremes and change on their food security and livelihoods, 
BECAUSE their exposure and vulnerability to hazards will be reduced and their adaptive 
capacity increased. The project will work to achieve this goal via activities under three 
interlinked components:  
(a) 
Component 1: Government, civil society and communities are strengthened to support 
local resilience to climate change impacts, including by providing access to climate 
information and early warning 
(b) 
Component 2: Scalable, locally appropriate actions are implemented to meet community 
adaptation needs to create climate-resilient, sustainable development pathways 
(adaptation packages) 
(c) 
Component 3: Institutional adaptive capacity is enhanced by building adaptive 
governance systems at the local level and enhancing local-provincial-national linkages. 
5. 
Adaptation impact. The project is expected to have adaptation impact by contributing 
to increased climate-resilient sustainable development in a number of ways – strengthening 
adaptive capacity and reducing exposure of vulnerable communities to climate risks; increasing 
generation and use of climate information in decision-making; and strengthening institutional 
and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and disaster risk reduction. Component 
2 involves implementing adaptation packages for climate-resilient agriculture, fisheries and 
diversified/enhanced livelihoods resilient to the conditions of flooding, storm surges, 
salinization, drought and heatwaves (marine and terrestrial). Activities under components 1 
and 3 will reduce the vulnerability of farmers and fishers through the use of improved climate 
information services and early warning systems through Community Disaster and Climate 
Change Committees (CDCCCs) for disaster preparedness and response, as well as through the 
adoption of resilient food preservation and processing techniques and income diversification.  
6. 
The project is designed to directly increase the climate resilience of 90,157 people in 
19,556 households and 282 communities across all six provinces (33 per cent of the total 
population or 43 per cent of the rural population), with a focus on the most vulnerable and 
socially marginalized groups. Project interventions should result in substantial reductions in the 
risk of loss of lives and assets due to reduced exposure to extreme weather events, as well as a 
significant increase in the number of people adopting climate-resilient livelihoods options. They 
will also increase the number of households with year-round food security. A further 110,000

=== PAGE 119 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 119 
 
 
 
people will benefit indirectly from enhanced governance capacity, increasing the project’s total 
reach to 95 per cent of the rural population.  
1.2 
Paradigm shift potential                                                                    Scale: N/A 
7. 
The project aims to bring about a paradigm shift away from an agricultural system that 
is highly sensitive to climate change to climate-resilient food productive systems which ensure 
food and water security for farmers and fishers. Achieving reduced vulnerability and increased 
resilience requires a shift in agricultural and fisheries practices in Vanuatu. The project aims to 
achieve a transformation in the way adaptation actions are conceived and implemented, 
providing local communities with up-to-date climate information and projections; empowering 
them to make informed decisions about the nature, timing and type of adaptation actions they 
want to take; and resourcing them to follow through and feed results upward through 
governance structures to create a fundamental shift in the way adaptation is pursued. To date, a 
largely top-down approach to community-based adaptation has resulted in adaptation actions 
at the local level that are not community owned and, therefore, not consistently maintained or 
sustained after project funding concludes.  
8. 
In order to promote longer-term sustainability of any adaptation technologies provided 
to communities, the project will ensure that adaptation package actions and implementation 
align with and support existing government systems and processes. Importantly, the project 
will ensure that government systems and processes are reflective of local needs and have the 
capacity to support them by strengthening the current vision of decentralization. This is seen as 
a key paradigm shift in a country that has largely been governed under a centralized approach 
despite the dispersed and isolated population. Output 2.4 activities under component 2 will help 
targeted communities to diversify their livelihood options via supporting access to markets for 
agriculture/food commodities, creating new avenues for remote and rural communities to 
engage with private sector entities and increase the sustainability of project activities.  
9. 
The project also addresses scalability and replicability – it works with 29 of Vanuatu’s 
71 Area Councils and provides a model for village-level CDCCCs to guide both adaptation and 
disaster preparedness and response. According to the funding proposal, this model, with 
concomitant investments in early warning systems and Area Council planning and budgeting, 
“could be applied in all remaining communities in Vanuatu via a combination of domestic 
resources and future climate finance”. The independent TAP notes that the project does not 
itself establish a pathway to scaling up the approach across the whole country, and that such 
scaling up will in practice probably rely on follow-on official development assistance of one kind 
or another, given government’s small revenue base. On the positive side, component 3 of the 
project invests in knowledge-sharing and learning engagement between national and 
subnational government and has a specific output 3.2 for “Enhanced local-provincial-national 
linkages through knowledge management and creation of feedback loops”. This includes 
facilitating meetings between national and subnational government stakeholders, including 
non-governmental actors, to discuss actions to increase the emphasis on long-term community-
level priorities within national and subnational planning and budgeting processes. 
10. 
In terms of catalysing impact beyond a one-off investment, a project rooted in the 
agriculture and fisheries sectors would ideally seek to achieve market transformation that will 
ensure the maintenance and continued uptake of adaptation approaches and technologies at 
producers’ own cost post-project. In this regard, the VCCRP seeks to provide incentives for 
longer-term uptake of climate-resilient practices through helping producers to achieve 
increased profit margins through support to cash crop production, preservation and processing. 
Support for crop varieties resilient to climate change impacts, combined with investment in 
renewable-powered solar food dryers and fish freezers, and capacity development for 
preservation processes like salting and pickling, should significantly enhance yields and prevent

=== PAGE 120 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 120 
 
 
 
food losses, improving food security even in the face of climate change. However, facilitating 
access to markets for surpluses remains a major challenge.  
11. 
The funding proposal identifies as one of three major existing barriers to effective and 
sustainable locally led adaptation in Vanuatu “Low incentives for private sector entities to 
engage with remote rural communities to support increased market access and help diversify 
livelihoods”. The project aims to introduce new livelihoods commodities (dried fruits, dried 
nuts, smoked fish, shell crafting, copra, coffee) in least 140 of the 282 target communities, 
establishing 90 women-led producer groups to support communities in bringing agricultural 
and fisheries goods to market and facilitating at least six private sector partnerships to support 
access to distant markets for commodities (assumed at one per province). If these economic 
activities could be put on a sustainable business footing, with strengthened supply chains 
including buyer contracts in place by the end of the project, this would indicate that a significant 
paradigm shift has taken place whereby the agriculture and fisheries sectors have reached a 
point where investments in climate change adaptation have become self-sustaining. 
12. 
This is noted in the project’s exit strategy with its six elements, one of which is to “Foster 
sustainable value chains in the local market. Food surpluses from agriculture and fisheries…are 
expected to be generated due to improvements in agricultural and fisheries practices, including 
through access to improved CIS, as well as significantly reduced losses from excess production 
and improved handling of produce. Establishing the value chains ensures a small but steady 
supply of income to the community with which the operation and maintenance of the installed 
processing technologies can be supported over the long term. Once communities have realized 
the potential of marketing food surpluses, this may lead to autonomous growth without the 
need for further external financial support, especially through fostering of twinning 
arrangements between local producers and private enterprises in the food sector”.  
13. 
There are a number of structural constraints, however, to the engagement of 
downstream private sector actors to help to bring products to markets such as Port Vila and its 
tourism sector, or cross-continental export markets. Like other small Pacific island countries 
with limited natural resources and narrow-based economies, Vanuatu is physically detached 
from major markets, has a small population spread across many islands, with trade balance 
skewed towards imports. The funding proposal notes the “long distances and limited 
transportation options between the project sites and the main commercial and residential hubs 
of Vanuatu” and the “poor communication and transport networks within and between islands”, 
and expresses the view that the “combination of extreme remoteness from major markets, very 
small size, dispersion over vast tracts of the Pacific Ocean, and environmental fragility results in 
very high cost of production of goods and services by both the private and public sector, leading 
to an assessment that the public sector is likely to remain the main source of formal sector 
employment and driver of economic activity in most Pacific Island countries for the foreseeable 
future”.  
14. 
The independent TAP asked the project proponents if it is realistic to attract private 
sector buyers or transport middlemen with small and unpredictable volumes of relatively low-
value produce that must travel long distances to market. In response, the AE noted that, “against 
a challenging backdrop there are success stories of local producers reaching domestic and at 
times export markets. Many of these examples are in the tourism industry where tourism 
service providers, including cruise ships, will purchase and use locally produced goods to 
support the local economy as part of the experience…they are selling”. It also noted that “a 
number of organizations and donors have been undertaking research in private sector and 
value chain analysis in Vanuatu over the years” and “a commonality across all studies and past 
and present projects is that it is not easy to engage the private sector, for the reasons outlined in 
the Funding Proposal. This is why we have purposely kept the target of this activity low (6) in 
comparison to the scale of the project.” The modest scale of the project’s ambitions in this

=== PAGE 121 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 121 
 
 
 
regard make sense and are realistic but limit the extent to which a market-based paradigm shift 
to sustainable adaptation practices is possible. 
15. 
An additional concern on the sustainability of project outcomes relates to the project’s 
lack of a costed operation and maintenance (O&M) plan with a commitment by government 
implementing partners, at the various levels at which adaptation technology will be employed, 
to meeting these costs. The independent TAP is concerned about how equipment purchased 
through the project will be sustained in terms of operating costs, upkeep and eventual 
replacement; for example, smartphones, tablets, etc., in activity 1.1.1, satellite dishes and solar 
panels in activity 1.3.1, earth auger and rotavator in activity 2.2.2, solar food driers and solar-
powered freezers in activity 2.4.1 and quad bikes in activity 3.1.2. The funding proposal 
acknowledges the importance of O&M, referring in the exit strategy to “inevitable on-going 
operation and maintenance (O&M) related expenses associated with introduced adaptation 
technologies (including solar PV freezers and solar air driers) to reduce the likelihood of 
negative externalities such as equipment falling into disrepair or breaking down” and noting 
that project activities include “training for target communities on installation, operation, 
maintenance and repair of all equipment, and budget to provide spare parts or training in using 
local materials for repairs and maintenance. This guidance on post-sale/installation O&M 
considerations will also be supplied to the [Department of Climate Change] DoCC on the 
selection and procurement of adaptation technology services for use in communities.”  
16. 
In response to a question from the independent TAP on ensuring that O&M needs will be 
adequately resourced, the AE stated that “Options for resourcing the O&M will depend on the 
needs and cost. The two principal options that will be used are: 1) government funding through 
development expenditure incorporated into government budgets along with the wider 
adaptation options as described in the project’s sustainability plan (for example, for the 
maintenance of quad bikes); and 2) the collection of maintenance fees from users at the point of 
use of the equipment in the communities (such as for the rotavators and freezers)”. In response 
to follow-up questions, the AE acknowledged the importance of formal O&M planning and 
committed itself to continuing to work with the co-executing entities on this to enhance project 
sustainability: “The approach chosen and used will be discussed and agreed with CDCCCs, Area 
Councils, Provinces and relevant line ministries to ensure the approach is consultative and 
participatory, as well as technically robust and reflects the needs and desires of the end users – 
the communities”.   
1.3 
Sustainable development potential                                             Scale: N/A 
17. 
Environmental co-benefits. In terms of the environment, the project is expected to 
have extensive co-benefits. On the marine side, the project seeks to increase the sustainability of 
ecosystem services by reducing reliance on vulnerable resources (e.g., reef fish) as a primary 
food source in rural coastal communities. Sustainable fisheries practices and coastal zone 
management will lead to the implementation of protections for local coastal habitats (including 
reduced mangrove clearing, limited reef gleaning); establishing new or enhancing existing 
“tabu” areas for environmental conservation (including freshwater resources, reef fisheries); 
and rehabilitating key degraded coastal resources (including mangroves, seagrass meadows and 
coral reefs). For terrestrial ecosystems, the project will develop coastal and upland resource 
management plans; establish living barriers; support reforestation of damaged forests and 
water catchments; manage invasive vine species impacting watersheds; and establish new or 
strengthen existing community conservation areas.  
18. 
Social co-benefits. The project has a number of social co-benefits. Increasing social 
inclusion across all areas of community life – with a specific focus on ensuring that the voices of 
traditionally underrepresented groups (e.g., youth, people with a disability) are heard in 
climate-related decision-making forums. The project aims to increase broad community health

=== PAGE 122 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 122 
 
 
 
outcomes by ensuring that communities have more secure access to locally available nutritious 
foods. Community adaptation planning and enhanced disaster risk reduction and response will 
enable increasing access to education, by ensuring that fewer school days are lost in the 
aftermath of extreme weather events. The project is also transformative in its focus on 
promoting women’s equitable representation in project activities and enabling greater 
economic empowerment and participation in decision-making by women, youth and people 
with a disability. 
19. 
Economic co-benefits. The project works to increase the household incomes of rural 
communities through livelihoods diversification and increasing access to markets. Component 2 
includes support to 261 communities on utilizing climate-resilient food preservation techniques 
to prevent food spoiling and wastage, and support to 140 communities on new livelihoods 
commodities (see para. 11 above). Communities will be supported in diversifying into new 
agricultural/food commodities and value added products that deliver greater income 
generating opportunities, including identifying local value added commodities with market 
potential and supporting processing. In collaboration with the Vanuatu Chamber of Commerce 
and Industry, private sector partnerships will be identified and established to further support 
community-level enterprise and enhance access to markets, and, where possible, support 
producer groups in transitioning into micro or small enterprises (including support in 
identifying longer-term sources of finance for business development). 
20. 
Gender-sensitive development impact. The project promotes increasing gender 
equality across all areas of community life, with a specific focus on ensuring that women’s 
voices are heard in climate-related decision-making forums and that the gendered nature of 
climate change impacts is a key component of all climate and development planning at the 
community and province level. This includes leadership training targeted at women, as part of 
capacity development for CDCCC members under component 1 to identify climate change and 
disaster risks at the local level and liaise on their needs with the National Disaster Management 
Office. In particular, the focus of output 2.4 on women is noted, including project interventions 
to i) introduce or scale up women-led local solutions for food processing and preservation, 
including identifying the foods most suitable for preservation and provision of processing 
equipment (solar dryers and freezers) to communities; ii) support women in diversifying into 
new agricultural/food commodities and value added products that deliver greater income-
generating opportunities, including identifying local value added commodities with market 
potential and supporting processing; and iii) support women-led enterprises in accessing 
private partnership options to access new (distant) markets for value added products.  
21. 
The independent TAP welcomes the inclusion in the gender action plan of childcare 
provision during project implementation to facilitate women’s full participation in project 
activities and decision-making forums. In response to a question from the independent TAP 
about the budgetary implications, the AE explained that the project takes two approaches to 
address women’s needs for support with childcare to enable them to engage in the project, 
neither of which will require GCF funds. The first approach is providing childcare for specific 
periods through existing Early Childhood Care and Development Centers that increasingly exist 
in communities (supported by government and organizations such as Save the Children) or 
through community groups and churches. The second approach is “to take the project to the 
women where they already regularly congregate. CDCCCs are mandated to include women (and 
the project will ensure gender balance) and they are increasingly represented in other decision-
making forums. Women leaders engaged by the project will then consult and engage with other 
women in the community and church groups where they already congregate and socialize”. This 
is welcomed as good practice in getting the details right to ensure women’s full and effective 
participation in project activities. 
1.4 
Needs of the recipient  
 
               
                     Scale: N/A

=== PAGE 123 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 123 
 
 
 
22. 
Vanuatu has the highest score in the world on the global disaster risk index – a score of 
49.74, indicating a high degree of physical exposure, relative vulnerability and resultant risk of 
loss of life from natural hazards. This is intensified by the predicted impacts of climate change 
discussed above, combined with adaptation deficits that need to be addressed to build 
community resilience to these impacts. Financing this adaptation gap has posed a significant 
challenge in Vanuatu, which, despite its graduation in 2020 from least developed country to 
lower middle-income country status, remains highly aid dependent. While steadily increasing 
economic and social indicators over recent decades are a positive sign for Vanuatu’s climate 
resilience, gains remain unevenly distributed and the most vulnerable people and communities 
will require adaptation assistance for the foreseeable future, particularly given the country’s 
significant exposure to extreme weather events and other hazards. The economic impact of the 
coronavirus disease 2019 pandemic, with the collapse of the tourism sector and reduced tax 
receipts, combined with the loss and damage suffered from level 5 Tropical Cyclone Harold that 
hit the country in April 2020, pushed the domestic budget into deficit in 2020/21, and is likely 
to have set Vanuatu back to gross domestic product levels typical of least developed countries.  
23. 
Consultations in preparation for the project by the Ministry of Climate Change and Save 
the Children Vanuatu revealed significant climate-related impacts on agriculture and fisheries, 
and the ecosystems that support them. Domestic revenue remains insufficient to adequately 
fund essential services to a widely dispersed, largely rural population, and donor programmes 
are generally focused on immediate development priorities (e.g., economic growth, poverty 
reduction, health and education). Only a few initiatives focus on climate change or 
mainstreaming the management of climate change risks and the building of resilience building 
into production and governments sectors. While the Government of Vanuatu has access to 
strong technical climate change capacity, the capacity to deliver adaptation assistance to the 
local level – in ways that meet community needs and expectations – remains restricted. 
Communities in Vanuatu urgently need scaled up support to address immediate adaptation 
priorities and to support the integration of climate change into local planning processes.  
1.5 
Country ownership 
 
 
 
 
                     Scale: N/A 
24. 
The project is well aligned with Vanuatu’s policy framework, including its first and 
subsequent nationally determined contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement, which 
highlight adaptation to climate change as the country’s overriding concern. The focus of the 
project is aligned with the national adaptation programme of action (NAPA) and focuses on 
three of the five identified adaptation priority areas, namely: agriculture and food security; 
community-based marine resource management; and integrated water resource management. 
The project is also aligned with the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy for Land-
Based Resources (2012–2022), the Disaster Risk Reduction and Disaster Management National 
Action Plan and the Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy (2016–2030). 
25. 
Vanuatu’s NAPA, Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy and GCF country 
programme are also fully aligned with the country’s National Sustainable Development Plan, the 
highest public policy framework in Vanuatu, which explicitly includes climate change adaptation 
as a key sustainable development priority. Policy objectives include: “Enhance traditional 
agricultural practices, focusing on disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation”; 
“Institutionalize climate change and disaster risk governance, and build institutional capacity 
and awareness”; “Improve monitoring and early warning systems”; “Strengthen post-disaster 
systems in planning, preparedness, response and recovery”; “Promote and ensure strengthened 
resilience and adaptive capacity to climate related, natural and man-made hazards”; and “Access 
available financing for climate change adaptation and disaster risk management”. 
26. 
Projected actions by the Government of Vanuatu on national priorities for climate 
change adaptation in 2021–2022 include efforts identified under its updated NDC (2020–2030)

=== PAGE 124 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 124 
 
 
 
to provide for quantifiable adaptation targets. The updated NDC includes specific targets and 
indicators aimed at enhancing the resilience of the agriculture and water sectors, developed 
with the objectives of maintaining food, income and water security. The overarching goal for the 
agriculture sector is: “Agriculture is able to support household income and food needs in a 
changing climate”. Two targets have been established to meet this goal, focused on small and 
medium-sized enterprises and private sector operators to generate sufficient income to cover 
essential household needs and services in normal and stressed (climate, disaster and 
environmentally) times; and implemented measures for enhancing the resilience of subsistence 
agriculture in a changing climate in the six provinces. The VCCRP aligns well with the NDC goal 
and targets.  
27. 
Vanuatu has had five GCF readiness proposals approved from 2015 to date, with which 
the VCCRP is well aligned. In 2018, the Government prepared the GCF country programme for 
Vanuatu, complementing the NAPA and Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy and 
assisting the government with the prioritization of adaptation investments. The VCCRP is 
highlighted as a priority in Vanuatu’s GCF country programme, including in the 2021 update. 
The funding proposal has been developed through close cooperation between the AE and the 
project’s two co-executing entities. The national designated authority and senior staff from key 
departments of the Ministry of Climate Change have been engaged in the design process, and the 
national designated authority will co-chair the Project Steering Committee, maintaining a role in 
the oversight of the project and direct lines of communication with the Project Management 
Unit throughout implementation. 
28. 
Country ownership has been promoted through an extensive and comprehensive 
consultation process with the Ministry of Climate Change and line ministries via workshops and 
meetings, as well as wider engagement with civil society. Relevant stakeholders were identified 
through a mapping exercise and consultations built on stakeholder engagement between 2016 
and 2019 with key partners, government and communities, to develop the concept note. 
Consultation was participatory and voluntary, working closely with primary stakeholders who 
will play a part in implementation. The continued consultation during the design phase with 
government at all levels, civil society and other relevant stakeholders further progressed the 
project development and ownership; it included discussions on the project concept and details 
of the design process, feasibility study results, focus of the adaptation package, validation of the 
target beneficiaries and approval of the full funding proposal at the meeting of the National 
Advisory Board on Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction in April 2021.   
1.6 
Efficiency and effectiveness 
 
 
                                 Scale: N/A 
29. 
The total project cost is USD 32.65 million, 80 per cent of which is a USD 26.18 million 
GCF grant contribution. The remaining USD 6.47 million, or 20 per cent of the total project cost, 
is made up of grant and in-kind contributions from the Government of Vanuatu (USD 1.54 
million), Save the Children Australia (USD 300,000) and the Australian Department of Foreign 
Affairs and Trade (USD 4.63 million). Government of Vanuatu contributions are in kind and are 
distributed across the three technical components of the project, but can be considered as 
significant given the country’s financial position as discussed above. The structure of the project 
as 100 per cent grant financed represents an appropriate level of concessionality in the 
country’s context, and the small profit margins expected on small-scale farming and fishing, 
combined with the public goods nature of ecosystem restoration, mean that the GCF project is 
not expected to crowd out potential private sector investments. 
30. 
The economic and financial analysis conducted for the project examined four specific 
adaptation actions deemed to be representative of the actions in the adaptation package menu, 
and found that all four have solid economic internal rates of return (EIRR) and can be justified 
on economic grounds: early warning system communications equipment (EIRR of 111 per

=== PAGE 125 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 125 
 
 
 
cent); nature-based solutions – coastal management (EIRR of 12 per cent); climate-resilient 
agriculture, with taro and yam as examples (EIRR of 19 per cent for taro and 27 per cent for 
yam); and food processing and storage (EIRR of 81 per cent).  
31. 
Looking at the project overall, however, the economic analysis showed the project has 
an EIRR of only 3 per cent, which, while positive, is below the discount rate used of 5 per cent. 
This is because of the high non-economically assessable costs (non-investment costs) required 
to enable and support the adaptation investments, and to create transformational change in 
remote and rural communities that are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The 
AE suggests that these costs are high because of the need to address significant capacity gaps 
and constraints at all levels (government at national, provincial and Area Council levels as well 
as at the community level) to ensure that the project leaves behind significantly increased 
institutional capacity to enhance the sustainability and replicability of the project’s adaptation 
investments and catalyse transformation. Also significant is the high cost of delivering projects 
in Pacific small island developing States because of their remoteness from major markets, small 
size and dispersion over vast tracts of the Pacific Ocean, such that the technical assistance and 
travel costs of projects in these island nations are typically higher than those in other 
developing regions. 
32. 
Application of best practice technologies is also important to ensure cost-effectiveness 
of the GCF and co-financing resources. In relation to the restoration of coastal ecosystems 
(mangrove swamps, seagrass beds and coral reefs), the project budget is fairly limited (USD 
385,000) and has no detailed explanation of how it will be spent, or which of the wide variety of 
restoration techniques, some extremely costly (particularly those involving diving), may be 
utilized. There are also additional complexities involved where cyclones have damaged coastal 
ecosystems extensively, for example, cutting off mangroves from the freshwater supply 
providing optimal salinity levels for survival.2 The feasibility report (Annex 2) also does not 
provide any reflection on various approaches, techniques and costings, and methods and which 
have proved effective and cost-effective in recent restoration initiatives, including in the Pacific.  
33. 
In response to a question from the independent TAP on this, the AE said that the budget 
highlighted is only for actual restoration work, and that there is additional budget in other 
activities for the assessment of the most appropriate sites, methodology and associated risks 
and costs. The development of local community adaptation plans during project implementation 
will include a feasibility assessment of the most appropriate rehabilitation techniques specific 
to that community. The AE also stated that “the project will also draw on established techniques 
in Vanuatu (e.g., coral gardening), as well as elsewhere in the Pacific (e.g. mangrove restoration) 
and globally (e.g., seagrass restoration) when selecting methodologies for specific sites and 
communities to suit the site conditions and are cost-effective”. 
34. 
In follow-up discussions, the independent TAP reiterated its concern, indicating that 
many coral reef and seagrass restoration techniques may be beyond the reach of the project’s 
budget, and that it was difficult to assess the potential of the proposed mangrove restoration 
when no information was provided about the current state of degradation of the mangroves, 
including whether engineering works might be needed to reconnect mangrove swamps to 
freshwater sources where such connections have been disturbed by cyclones or human 
activities, to avoid the scenario in which newly planted mangrove propagules die en masse. In 
response, the AE stated that many of the Vanuatu islands’ coastal ecosystems are still in a 
relatively undamaged state and would need smaller investments to maintain their functionality 
as climate change buffers. A detailed assessment of the ecosystem condition and what is needed 
to restore functioning will thus need to be the starting point for assessments of potential 
 
2 See the recent report by The Nature Conservancy: Hurricane Damages to Mangrove Forests and Post-Storm 
Restoration Techniques and Costs; available at 
https://www.nature.org/content/dam/tnc/nature/en/documents/TNC_MangroveHurricaneDamages_Report.pdf.

GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 126 
 
 
 
approaches and techniques. Detailed monitoring mechanisms will need to be put in place to 
track progress of restoration efforts, including resultant changes in ecosystem functioning that 
buffer climate change impacts.

35. 
The independent TAP recommends this funding proposal for approval, subject to one 
condition. In order to maximize sustainability of the project’s investments in equipment and 
technologies for adaptation, the independent TAP requests that an O&M plan be developed as a 
condition to first disbursement, as described below. Therefore, this endorsement by the 
independent TAP is subject to the following condition: 
Condition precedent to first disbursement by GCF under the funded activity agreement:  
(a) 
Prior to the first disbursement of GCF Proceeds for the Funded Activity, the accredited 
entity shall finalize and submit to the Fund a costed Operations and Maintenance (O&M) 
Plan for the project, in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat.

Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical 
Advisory Panel's assessment (FP184) 
Proposal name: 
Vanuatu community-based climate resilience project (VCCRP) 
Accredited entity:

Impact potential 
SCA welcomes ITAP’s positive assessment of the project’s impact potential. This reflects the 
project’s focus on building the resilience of some of the most vulnerable people and 
communities in Vanuatu, a small island developing state only recently graduated from least 
developed country status (and whose economy has been substantially impacted by the 
COVID-19 pandemic). The project will directly reach over 40% of the rural population and set 
the foundations for a step change in the way community-based adaptation is undertaken in 
Vanuatu.  
Paradigm shift potential 
SCA notes ITAP’s assessment of the project’s paradigm shift potential. We note the concerns 
expressed on the private sector impact and need for an operations and maintenance plan. As 
ITAP notes, the project’s modest aims for private sector engagement make sense and are 
realistic. The project will work within the constraints of a largely subsistence and widely 
dispersed economy to support enhanced market engagement among targeted communities to 
help diversify livelihoods strategies and increase their resilience to climate change impacts. 
Regarding the project’s operation and maintenance, we note the need to develop a costed 
O&M plan for the project. We accept this condition and will ensure it is met prior to first 
disbursement of project resources.   
Sustainable development potential 
SCA welcomes ITAP’s positive assessment of the project’s sustainable development potential, 
particularly in relation to the focus on addressing gender equity issues and supporting 
women’s economic empowerment through supporting women’s decision-making roles in 
local climate governance mechanisms and promoting resilient livelihoods diversification 
activities as this is a key focus of the project.  
Needs of the recipient 
SCA welcomes ITAP’s positive assessment of the needs of the recipients, reflecting the urgent 
need for scaled up adaptation assistance to highly vulnerable remote and rural communities.  
Country ownership

=== PAGE 128 ===
GCF/B.32/02/Add.01 
Page 128 
 
 
 
SCA welcomes ITAP’s positive assessment of the project’s country ownership. The project has 
been developed via a highly collaborative and participatory process, ensuing high ownership 
by the participating government ministries and targeted communities.  
Efficiency and effectiveness 
SCA notes ITAP’s assessment of the project’s efficiency and effectiveness.  Regarding the 
project’s overall EIRR, as the ITAP assessment notes, the significant non-economically 
assessable costs and high cost of delivery context result in a low overall EIRR. This is offset by 
the economic and financial analysis findings that each of the assessable activities have strong 
EIRR. Furthermore, the project responds to the GCF’s objective to build the climate resilience 
of the most vulnerable communities, which requires substantial investments in non-
economically assessable activities to ensure the sustainability of project outcomes. We 
believe this investment in capacity building, its focus on highly climate vulnerable 
communities and its sustainability focus, along with the experience of the executing entities 
in delivering community-level projects will result in a highly efficient and effective project.  
Regarding ITAP’s concern around the ecosystems-based adaptation component, the 
community-level bio-physical assessments will be undertaken prior to any decisions being 
made on specific adaptation activities related to restoration of coastal ecosystems. These 
assessments will feed into the broader participatory community adaptation planning process 
to ensure high local ownership of resulting activities.  
We note ITAP’s assessment that ‘application of best practice technologies is also important to 
ensure cost-effectiveness of the GCF and co-financing resources’. We agree, however we 
would highlight here that best practice must also include a focus on the local context. The 
technologies that will be utilized under this project will support communities to achieve their 
adaptation objectives in a way that sits well in the local context. The project is also promoting 
best practice in processes, for example, support for highly participatory local adaptation 
planning processes and putting the principles for locally-led adaptation into practice.   
Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel: 
SCA welcomes ITAP’s overall positive assessment of the funding proposal and notes with 
gratitude the recommendation that the Board approves the Project.  
We note and accept the proposed condition on the development of a costed operations and 
maintenance plan for the project prior to first disbursement.  
As outlined above, we believe this project will have a transformative impact on the climate 
resilience of highly vulnerable remote and rural communities in the small island developing 
state of Vanuatu, who, through no fault of their own, are facing a context of escalating climate 
extremes which, unaddressed, will likely undermine their sustainable development potential. 
Remote and rural communities in SIDS face substantial adaptation capacity and finance gaps. 
We believe this project will showcase how scaling up locally-led approaches to adaptation 
and building local capacity to access and effectively utilise future flows of climate finance can 
help close both these gaps and provide a model for other SIDS with similar challenges.

Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project 
Gender Assessment and Action Plan
2 
Part 1 VCCRP Gender and Social Inclusion Analysis/Assessment 
Introduction 
1. The project aims to improve the adaptive capacities of rural communities and recognises that within
communities’ gender, age and disability influence adaptation capacities and that addressing structural
disadvantages is a precondition to adaptation.  This analysis considers the ways in which gender, age
and disability intersect with climate change and disasters in Vanuatu with a focus on rural communities.
The analysis sets out key data and analysis across six domains and includes recommendations for the
Project. The core approach is to embed gender equality and social inclusion across the Project activities
and operations. The Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan highlights actions included in the Project
wide logical framework.
2. The six domains of analysis are:
•
Law, policy and institutions.
•
Social norms and beliefs.
•
Roles, responsibilities and time use.
•
Decision making, leadership and participation.
•
Access to and control of resources; and
•
Safety, dignity and well-being.
3. The analysis and action plans are based on literature review, key informant interviews with national
government officials, and community profiles in North Ambrym, Epi and North West Santo, undertaken
through community meetings, surveys and women’s focus group discussions.
Methodology 
The assessment and action plan was prepared by the feasibility team located in Vanuatu and the GESI 
adviser based in Australia. The team used a combination of primary and secondary methods: 
•
Literature review of policy documents, project reports and published research
•
Women’s focus group discussions and community surveys in 8 communities across three provinces,
using standard question guides and in local languages.
•
Key informant interviews with national government staff including Ministry of Climate Change, Ministry
of Agriculture, Livestock, Fisheries, Forests and Biosecurity; Department of Women’s Affairs and
Department of Local Authorities.
•
Key informant interviews with disability NGOs.
4. Key Findings
•
National policies on climate change, agriculture, fisheries and gender equality recognise the
importance of gender equality and social inclusion in building community climate and disaster
resilience
•
Translating these policies into service delivery is hampered by limited expertise in gender
mainstreaming at national, provincial and area levels of government. Support is needed for area and
province level staff to undertake gender and social inclusion analysis with communities and integrate
findings into annual planning, budgeting and reporting systems
•
At family level women and men report a high level of shared decision making. Men are seen as heads
of households and social norms privilege male decision making in traditional, formal and community
settings. At community level NGO’s have supported inclusive decision making in Community Disaster
and Climate Change Committees (CDCCCs) which has created space and shifted norms around
participation of women, people with disability and youth in decision making.
•
Under customary law women’s entitlement to land and natural resources are mediated through
husbands and sons. This has significant implications for economic development, food security and
gender-based violence.
•
In rural communities while women and men are equally active producers in agriculture and fisheries,
the majority of extension and training programs are tailored to the needs and interests of male
producers.

=== PAGE 132 ===
Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project 
Gender Assessment and Action Plan
3 
•
Women and girls work significantly more hours than men, with less time for leisure or rest.  In rural
Tanna women worked twice as many hours as men. Impacts of climate change such as drought,
heavy rains, sea level rise and rough seas all increased women’s workloads.
•
Service providers need to shift approach to recognise that rural livelihoods are family enterprises, with
women making up half of rural producers. Engaging families rather than just male heads of household
using the family teams approach is an effective way of reaching women and men, recognising and
building on the work of all family members
•
Already high levels of gender-based violence are amplified in the aftermath of disasters, due to
displacement, breakdowns in services and increased pressure on families with loss of food stores and
access to water.
Key Data 1 
•
The population of Vanuatu is 272,459 (Male 138, 265/Female 134,194)
•
The annual population growth rate is 2.3%, one of the highest rates in the world.
•
Vanuatu is one of only two countries in the world where there are no women in Parliament.2
•
Vanuatu’s HDI score is 0.609 (2019) which put the country in the medium human development
category – positioning it at 140 out of 189 countries and territories. Between 2005 and 2019 Vanuatu’s
HDI increased by 6.1% (Vanuatu Human Development Report). The Gender Development Index
which provides the sex-disaggregated data of the three dimensions measured in the HDI was not able
to be calculated in 2019 due to a lack of data. Similarly, the Gender Inequality Index for Vanuatu has
not been measured.3
•
The country has a young population with 39% under 15 years of age, and 26 % aged 15-29. Life
expectancy for women is 72.2 years and 69 years for men.
•
Three quarters of ni-Vanuatu live in rural areas and rely on agriculture, marine and forest resources.
•
12.7% of the population live below the National Poverty line.4 57% of people below the poverty line
live in rural areas and poverty is concentrated amongst female headed households.
•
The disability prevalence rate is estimated at 2.6%, with 22% of the population reporting some level
of difficulty in functioning5. Rates of disability are higher in rural areas and increase with age. There
is no difference in disability prevalence between women and men.
•
The infant mortality (death< 1 year) is 28/1,000 births6 while 28.9% of children under 5 are stunted7.
•
Early marriage is widespread, 22% of women aged 20 – 24 were first married before 18. 17% of girls
in rural areas aged 15-19 have started childbearing.
•
The maternal mortality rate is 72/100,000 live births, 8% of deaths of females of reproductive age are
maternal deaths8.
•
60% of women have experienced sexual or physical violence
•
The literacy rate is 88.3% for men, 86.7% for women. 80% of women in rural areas reported being
literate compared with 98% of women in urban areas9. People with disability are less than half as
likely to read or write Bislama or other language compared with people without disability. Women with
disability less than half as likely to read or write compared men with disability.10
•
The school enrolment rate of girls as a percentage of that of boys was 95.2 at the primary level and
101.5 at the secondary level (UNICEF 2013). The 2016 Mini Census reported 11% of children aged
6-13 years were not attending school and most of these children were in rural areas.

Gender Assessment and Action Plan
4 
•
Children with disability are up to 10 times less likely to attend school11 with girls more disadvantaged
than boys: In 2013 15% of girls and 28% of boys with disability had ever attended school.12
•
The majority of people in Vanuatu rely on subsistence income, with little difference in participation
rates between rural men 47% and women 44%.
•
Women constitute 49.5% of agricultural producers and spend more time than rural men on agricultural
production, income generation and household work. Women’s incomes are on average lower than
men’s incomes. 13
•
40% of women declared unpaid family work as their main employment compared with 20% of men.
•
There were no women elected to the Vanuatu National Parliament in 2020, a situation that has
persisted for three terms. Since independence in 1980 five women have been elected to Parliament.
•
At subnational level a quota system introduced in 2013 has led to increased number of women elected
or appointed to municipal and provincial councils.14
•
The World Economic Forum Global Gender Gap Report, 2021 saw Vanuatu as the lowest performer
in Asia Pacific region in closing gender gaps. Since 2006 (since WEF started reporting) no progress
has been made in closing gender gaps.15
Laws, Policy and Institutional Arrangements 
5. The Constitution of Vanuatu (1980) states that people have the right to live freely and without
discrimination of the grounds of sex, race, place of origin, religious or traditional beliefs, political opinions
or language. The Constitution also acknowledges special measures to benefit females, children, young
persons and member of under privileged groups and inhabitants of less developed areas.  Vanuatu is
signatory to key international human rights treaties: Convention on the Elimination and Discrimination
Against Women (ratified 1995); the Convention on the Rights of the Child (ratified 1993) and Convention
on the Rights of People with Disability (ratified 2008). In 2011 Vanuatu signed the United Nations joint
statement on ending acts of violence and related human rights violations based on sexual orientation and
gender identity. Vanuatu is also signatory to regional commitments in the Pacific Leaders Gender Equality
Declaration and the Pacific Platform for Action on Gender Equality and Women’s Human rights (2018-
2030).
6. The National Sustainable Development Plan (2016 – 2030) sets out specific policy objectives for gender
equality and social inclusion in education (2.1), health (3.1), government planning and budgeting (4.1),
protection from violence and discrimination (4.2), and empowering people with disabilities and improving
accessibility (4.3, 4.5). 16
7. The country is preparing to launch the second National Gender Equality Policy (NGEP 2020 - 2030).
Implementation of the first National Gender Equality Policy (2015 – 2019) delivered gains against strategic
objectives of enhancing women’s economic status; mainstreaming gender equality into national sectoral
policies (and planning to some extent); and advancing efforts to reduce gender-based violence and
increase women’s participation in leadership and decision making. The new NGEP includes an additional
strategic area: Fostering gender-responsive and community-driven solutions to climate and disaster
resilience. The addition of this strategic area reflects experience from TC Pam that addressing disaster
preparedness and response at community and household level is an effective entry point for shifting social
norms about gender and disability.17
8. Priority actions in the NGEP relevant to building climate and disaster resilience cover job creation and
skills development for women in agriculture, fisheries, handicrafts and tourism, including women with
disabilities (2.2.2); Enhancing rural women’s access to markets, land, credit and financial literacy training
(2.2.3); Increasing women’s participation in decision making processes and leadership including in
emergencies (3.1.3 and 5.3.1) Engaging women with disabilities in leadership and decision making roles
(3.1.4); increasing capacity of climate change and disaster governance bodies (CDCCCs, area and

Gender Assessment and Action Plan 
 
5 
provincial councils) to plan for and respond to needs of vulnerable groups (5.1.2); allocation of budgets 
for gender analysis and specific gender activities ( 5.1.3);  Funding and supporting women led initiatives 
that contribute to adaptation (5.3.2); Embedding gender and social inclusion outcomes in tourism and 
productive sector recovery (5.3.3). Provincial gender action plans have been developed with specific 
priorities drawn from the NGEP.  
9. Vanuatu’s National Disability Inclusive Development policy (2018 – 2025) commits to mainstreaming the 
Rights of Persons with Disabilities to ensure that development efforts meet the particular needs and 
priorities of persons with disabilities and that targeted disability specific services are made available. The 
policy also commits to improving data collection, coordination between sectors, enhancing access, raising 
awareness of the rights of people with disabilities and providing support specifically to women and girls 
with disabilities. 
10. To date the Ministry of Justice and Community Services18 has lead responsibility for women children and 
disability. The Ministry’s Department for Women’s Affairs (DWA), Child Desk and Disability Desk are 
responsible for policy and program development and supporting mainstreaming into other parts of 
government, however, are under resourced. Annual budgets only cover staff costs and not programming, 
and technical expertise to support mainstreaming across government is limited. DWA has 14 staff 
including one in each province. DWA plays a convening role across the desks including leading the 
Gender and Protection Cluster at national level, with subgroups on children, disability and leadership. The 
Cluster is active in both peacetime and response and with the establishment of DWA in provinces efforts 
to build provincial gender and protection capabilities are a priority.  
11. Sectoral policies for climate change and disaster risk reduction (CCDRR Policy)19;  agriculture, fisheries, 
livestock and forestry20 all include language on gender equality and inclusion. The CCDRR policy 
specifies participation of women, youth and people with disability in Community Disaster and Climate 
Change Committees (CDCCCs). The other policies address extension and training services, 
representation in decision making, as well employment opportunities in departments. In 2017 the Council 
of Ministers identified five priority ministries for gender-based budgeting:  agriculture/fisheries, climate 
change, lands, education and local authorities.  
12. The legal and policy framework provides a solid basis and direction for mainstreaming gender equality 
and social inclusion in climate and disaster resilience action. The CCDRR, agriculture, fisheries, forestry 
and livestock policies do not specify how departments should go about gender and social inclusion work 
or set indicators or targets to generate action and accountability. A recent FAO assessment of the 
enabling environment for gender equality in the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock, Fisheries, Forests and 
Biosecurity (MALFFB) found that there is substantial staff interest in understanding more about gender 
equality and social inclusion and how to translate this into their work. The gap is in financial and human 
resources to implement gender mainstreaming.21 MALFFB has extension officers in all six provinces and 
is supported by the Vanuatu Agricultural and Research and Technical Centre and the Vanuatu Agricultural 
College, neither of which have specific programmes targeting women/girls or people with disabilities.22 
13. Support is required for departments to translate policy into practice and accelerate gender-based planning 
and budgeting as per the Council of Ministers decision in 2017. Technical resources are needed to 
support departments to undertake gender and social inclusion analysis and integrate findings into annual 
planning, budgeting and reporting, at national and subnational levels.  
Social Norms and Beliefs 
14. Social norms and expectations shape the ways in which different groups can access information about 
climate change and disasters and how they can adapt and respond. In Vanuatu social norms give men 
control over women’s behaviour and privilege male decision making in traditional, formal, community 
settings. Gender based violence is widespread and continues to be accepted as a way of enforcing 
expectations and exerting power.23,24 People with disabilities face stigma and isolation underpinned by 
negative beliefs and shame about disability.  
 
18 In late 2020 the Government moved to abolish the Ministry of Justice and Community Services and absorb functions into other Ministries, this change is currently being contested.

Vanuatu Community-based Climate Resilience Project 
Gender Assessment and Action Plan 
 
6 
15. In rural areas, kastom governance is the main form of community organisation and social protection 
(Vanuatu has no non-contributory formal social welfare programs).25 Kastom is usually patrilineal with 
male chiefs presiding. While women go to chiefs when facing abuse or family conflict, family and 
community are prioritised above individuals in decision making. Beliefs and practices vary widely between 
islands but are widely observed and co-exist with people’s strong faith in Christianity. Kastom has 
changed over time with foreign contact, devaluing the role of women in decision making. According to the 
NGEP 2020, “Whilst kastom bestowed authority to men with chiefly titles, decisions were shared and 
agreed to by both spouses within the extended family unit. The role played by women in household 
decision-making no longer exist or constitute modern notions of kastom.”26 Community profiles undertake 
in the feasibility stage of this project indicate a more nuanced picture at household level with women 
reporting more shared decision making.  
16. Social norms towards early marriage and childbearing are persistent especially in rural areas. 22% of 
women aged 20 to 24 years were first married or in union before age 18.27 17% of girls in rural areas (15-
19) have started childbearing compared with 12% in urban areas.28 Opportunities for education, 
employment, mobility are all the more restricted once childbearing starts. Unmarried women and girls live 
with their families and move to their husband’s home on marriage, to a different community or island, 
away from social supports. A woman’s entitlement to land and other natural resources is through her 
husband and sons, leaving women vulnerable if she is unmarried, the relationship breaks down or her 
husband dies, and she has no sons.  Women with disability are three times as likely to be widowed or 
divorced as compared with men with disability and nine times more likely to have never married as 
compared with women without disability.29 This has direct impact on access to land and resources for 
women with disability.  
17. There are significant taboos associated with menstruation, internalised by women and girls including the 
belief that menstruating women and girls will kills crops if they touch them, they must not work in gardens, 
cook food or lift heavy objects. They must also collect their own water for bathing and washing their 
reusable menstrual products, wash their own menstrual products and use separate toilets and bathing 
shelters.30  
18. People with disability in Vanuatu face significant stigma and isolation due to negative social norms and 
beliefs. Parents can be ashamed of sending children with disability into public so do not send them to 
school31, particularly where children experience intellectual disability. People with disability are seen only 
as vulnerable and excluded from community decision making. Government and CSO programs on food 
security, water security and livelihoods are not targeted to people with disabilities and they are unable to 
access mainstream opportunities.32 Vanuatu has no non-contributory social welfare programs.  
Roles, Responsibilities and Time Use 
19. Social norms and expectation underpin roles and responsibilities for women and men and girls and boys. 
Rural families operate as a family unit to undertake productive work. Women are expected to care for all 
family members manage food gardens, fishing and water collections for family consumption, host guests, 
weave mats and handicrafts, sell produce at markets and also provide support to local community and 
church. Men have greater mobility and are engaged in outside employment and politics, as well as 
community and church activity. Both women and men are engaged in agriculture in almost equal numbers 
though they may grow different crops and use different levels of technology.33 Generally women tend to 
have “lighter” agricultural roles of weeding, planting and maintaining crops closer to home, while men take 
on “heavier” roles of clearing and ploughing land including use of machinery. Fishing in deeper water with 
larger vessels for fish for sale is a predominantly male role, while women engage in coastal gleaning and 
fishing from canoes for food security.  Women also participate in aquaculture and fish processing to 
generate income.34  Social norms associated with who is a fisher tend to focus on male roles in deeper 
water, neglecting the role of women who harvest close to shore. This both underestimates the impact of 
 
25 Vanuatu has no non-contributory formal social protection pensions or payments. The Vanuatu National Provident Fund is a contributory fund open to formal and informal workers, 
however only 10 % of rural women and 18% of rural men have an account FA) pg41.

fishing pressure along the coast and undervalues the contributions of women.35 Few fisheries programs 
benefit women or transformed gender relations, and inclusive approaches are hampered by limited 
knowledge and capacity of fisheries managers and institutional cultures.36  
20. People with disabilities are equally likely to take part in productive activities as people without disability 
but are less likely to be employed outside the home due to barriers to education, training and employment. 
37 Information on specific roles of people with disability in agriculture or fisheries in Vanuatu is not 
available.  
21. Overall women and girls work significantly more hours than men, with less time for leisure or rest. FAO 
rural case studies of time use in Tanna showed women worked twice as many hours as men.38 When 
faced with this data men in the community were surprised and many indicated a willingness to take on 
more work so there is better balance in families. Climate changes including drought, heavy rains, rough 
seas and sea level rise increase women’s workloads in a number of ways:  reducing productivity of 
household gardens; reducing productivity of materials for handicraft production; increasing time and 
distance for water collection and reducing opportunity for gleaning and fishing. Communities in North 
Ambryn, Epi and West Santo all experience three or more months water shortages, requiring greater 
travel to collect drinking water and increasing incidence of water borne diseases. After disasters (and 
during the pandemic)39 women’s care burden increases substantially with closure of kindergartens and 
schools, loss of homes and gardens and water sources.  
22. Labour migration is an increasingly important feature of rural livelihoods as the need for cash grows. Most 
labour migrants are men, moving to urban centres or Australia and New Zealand for seasonal agricultural 
or other low or semi-skilled work. Women from rural areas participate in migration to urban areas to work 
in domestic roles in extended family or kinship groups. Migration generates substantial remittances that 
are used for family and productive purposes such as school fees, health care, improving housing and 
water supply, purchasing boats and machinery. Migration also creates pressure on families with increased 
work burdens on women left to care for children, elderly and family members with disability. Women have 
greater autonomy and financial decision-making power while male partners are away, and relationship 
tensions increase both during separation and on return.  
23. Women and girls’ roles and workloads restrict access to training and extension initiatives that could assist 
with adaptation strategies such as livelihood diversification. When family incomes are under pressure the 
needs of girls and family members with disability are the first to be compromised with reduced spending 
on rehabilitation and healthcare, withdrawal of girls from school and early marriage. While primary and 
secondary school participation rates have improved, on marriage girls and women’s ability to participate 
in further education and training is restricted.  
24. Agricultural extension services and enterprise development training at local level tends to be targeted to 
male heads of household, with men’s work valued over women’s work. Service providers need to shift 
approach to recognise that rural livelihoods are family enterprises, with women making up half of rural 
producers. Engaging families rather than just male heads of household is an effective way of reaching 
women and men, recognising and building on the work of all family members.40 The family farm teams 
approach combines technical extension/training with modules to identify family goals, roles, how work 
burdens can be shared, and improving decision making and communication.41 The approach has been 
pioneered by CARE Australia in PNG and is being used with success across Melanesia. The family farm 
teams approach can also address the increased risks of gender-based violence that women can face 
when they increase their incomes.42 Building on women’s existing roles including targeting capacity 
support to women in markets and in women’s church groups are also entry points.

Decision Making, leadership and participation 
25. Decision making is male dominated in Vanuatu. Since independence in 1980 only five women have been 
elected to Vanuatu’s national parliament, and there are no women in Parliament in 2021, While women 
have competed in election none have been successful in the last three elections. At subnational level a 
quota system introduced in 2013 has led to increases in the number of women elected or appointed to 
Municipal and Provincial councils where women now make up about one third of members. While there 
are no women mayors there are some women in deputy mayor roles. In the public sector women are 
better represented in leadership.  Two of seven director generals are women (notably in in the MCC and 
MJCS) and 8/43 directors were women in 2019.43 At the international level women have led Vanuatu 
delegations to the UNFCCC COP (2017 and 2019) and women’s representation in delegations has been 
30-50% since 2017.   
26. Social norms about masculinity and leadership are slowly changing. At community level while male chiefs 
head the kastom governance structure, community committees for climate and disasters, water and 
village development provide a framework for women, people with disability and youth to participate and 
take on leadership roles particularly when supported by NGOs. Activities to build confidence and skills of 
women, people with disability and youth have increased community acceptance of their leadership 
capabilities.  
27. The Vanuatu Gender and protection cluster was established in 2014 and has built a strong network of 
government, international and national NGO partners focused on disaster management, as well as 
disaster risk reduction and climate action. The national cluster is chaired by DWA with NGO support, and 
includes subcommittees on children, leadership and disability. The cluster has been an important venue 
for building skills, awareness and systematic tools for more inclusive disaster preparedness and 
response. Challenges faced by the Cluster include securing financing from government for program 
implementation, influencing mainstreaming in other clusters and building provincial cluster mechanisms. 
There is a risk that action on gender and protection is left only to the gender and protection cluster or 
DWA, rather than embedded into the operations of all clusters, provincial and municipal emergency 
operations.  
28. Women have low levels of representation in the formal private sector but are active in small and medium 
enterprises based on agriculture, handicrafts and food production. Market associations and loans and 
savings cooperatives have large numbers of women members including in leadership roles. These 
provide an important entry point for business skills development (eg UN Women Markets for Change, 
VANWODs financial literacy and savings and loans groups) but have not yet been targeted for climate 
adaptation and resilience building. While women in most communities are excluded from kastom 
leadership roles they do play an active part in church communities including leadership of women’s groups 
and youth groups. 
29. At the household level men are seen as the head of household. In the FAO Tanna case study women 
reported many decisions are made by women and men together, but that men are the final decision 
makers. In Women’s Focus Groups in North Ambryn and Epi, North West Santo women reported that 
most decision making was shared between women and men. Project interventions using a family 
approach are important to reinforce existing shared decision making, instead of privileging male decision 
making.  
30. People with disability report isolation and stigma, as well lack of accessibility as barriers that prevent 
participation in community events and committees. Examples include CDCCC meetings being held in 
“open” settings that people with disabilities cannot access, or fear shame and ridicule.44 In some cases 
teachers take on the role of representing people with disability instead of communities supporting people 
with disability themselves to have voice. NGO and CSO activism by Vanuatu Disability Promotion and 
Advocacy Association, Vanuatu Society for People with Disability and Rainbow Theatre (Won Smal Bag) 
and the Vanuatu Civil Society Disability Network is challenging this exclusion. After TC Harold there was 
recognition by government and donors that data systems needed to improve to better support people with 
disabilities in disasters.45 The Vanuatu Skills Partnership is also demonstrating change employing seven 
people with disability as trainers, and 42% of industry trainers in the productive sectors are women. 46 
 
43 Vanuatu Government, NGEP 2020 – 2030 (draft)  
44 UNDP, USAID Building the Resilience of Persons with Disabilities to Cope with Climate Change in the Asia Pacific Region, Vanuatu Country Report 2019 
45 A new program to improve province and area level disability data through the Ministry of health information management system is being designed with support from; SPC.
